#131. Environmentalists criticise choice of Earthshot prize venue owned by fossil fuel firm

Mumbai facility is part of Reliance Industries, owner of world’s biggest oil refinery and funder of controversial mega-zoo

The Prince of Wales’s Earthshot prize will host its forthcoming annual awards ceremony at a venue owned by the billionaire Ambani family conglomerate, drawing criticism from conservationists over their fossil fuel businesses and their foundation’s controversial mega-zoo.

A spokesperson for the Earthshot prize, which awards £1m to five winners every year to invest in conservation work, confirmed to the Guardian and the news outlet Mongabay that this year’s ceremony will be held at the Jio World Convention Centre in Mumbai on 17 November.

The venue is part of Mukesh Ambani’s Reliance Industries, which also owns Jamnagar, the world’s biggest oil refinery in Gujarat on India’s western coast. The group’s philanthropic arm funds the 1,400-hectare (3,500-acre) private zoo Vantara, which was founded by Mukesh’s son Anant Ambani and inaugurated by the Indian prime minister, Narendra Modi, last year. The zoo is embroiled in a long-running controversy over its vast collection of more than 150,000 animals, which reportedly includes some of the world’s most endangered species – from the radiated tortoise, native to Madagascar, to the Spix’s macaw, endemic to north-eastern Brazil but now considered extinct in the wild.

Megan Carr, of the Wildlife Animal Protection Forum South Africa (WAPFSA), warned that using a venue owned by Reliance “could lend an unearned environmental prestige to a corporate group currently under question by the global conservation community”.

Conservation groups, including WAPFSA, have raised concerns about the speed with which Vantara has amassed so many animals, and about how they were sourced. The site, near the family’s oil refinery, houses more than 2,000 species of animals.

This week, the 32-member committee that governs Cites, the UN-administered body that regulates trade in endangered species, requested that India submit another report on the steps it is taking to address concerns relating to its imports of protected species.

The committee also requested the Cites secretariat review 11 cases where there are unresolved questions over how animals were sent to Vantara.

Joanna Swift, the executive director of communications at the Earthshot prize, said it had not sought a philanthropic partnership for its award ceremony venue and that it was paying the standard commercial rate to rent the space.

“The [Jio World Convention Centre] was selected because it allowed us to host the event in Mumbai while meeting the practical requirements of an event of this size. It offers world-class facilities, technical infrastructure and experience of hosting major international events, including environmental ones,” Swift said. “In February this year, JWCC hosted the first ever Mumbai climate week.”

Vantara, Kensington Palace and JWCC have been approached for comment.

Vantara has previously denied allegations of wildlife trafficking and says its purpose is “to rescue wild animals in distress, provide treatment along with lifelong care, and rehabilitate those fit for the wild”.

“With regulatory approval, we also breed select endangered or extinct-in-the-wild species, serving as a modern Noah’s Ark to ensure their survival under human care and restore wild populations through reintroduction,” Vantara says on its website.

Last year, India’s supreme court cleared the zoo of allegations that animals had been acquired illegally or mistreated.

A later Cites fact-finding mission raised concerns about India’s compliance with international rules on animal imports in 2025. It recommended temporary restrictions on India, although the recommendation was later withdrawn.

Susan Cheyne, the vice-chair of the International Union for Conservation of Nature’s small apes section, said that Vantara’s model has not yet been shown to support conservation and “may be driving demand for wild animals in trade”.

The planet's most important stories. Get all the week's environment news - the good, the bad and the essential

“For gibbons and other primates, this matters a great deal. Moving animals out of their range countries into a private facility does nothing for wild populations unless there is a transparent, credible plan for those animals: for welfare, for any conservation breeding with clear goals, and for return to range states where that is possible. Real rescue and sanctuary work for primates is done by underfunded centres in range countries,” Cheyne said.

Daniela Freyer, co-founder of the German NGO Pro Wildlife, added: “Our planet is increasingly threatened by the overexploitation of wildlife and by climate change driven by fossil fuels. This week, the Cites Standing Committee, which oversees international wildlife trade, decided to keep India under its compliance procedure, as questions remain about the origin of some of the world’s rarest animals imported to Vantara. A prize whose goals include protecting nature and fixing our climate should be careful not to lend its reputation to these questions.”

Vantara had sought to lobby the UK, the chair of this week’s Cites meetings, attempting to persuade officials to take a more sympathetic view of India’s handling of imports to Vantara, the Guardian and Mongabay reported earlier this month. Ministers were advised not to engage formally with Vantara, internal documents revealed.

The Earthshot prize, founded by Prince William in 2020, is billed as the most prestigious environmental award. William appeared on the BBC’s Graham Norton show last month to promote the prize, saying it was designed to “talk about urgency and optimism together”.

Swift said that it was vital to host an edition of the Earthshot prize in India.

“Mumbai is the sixth largest city on earth. It is home to exceptional environmental solutions and the people behind them, with an impact felt in India and around the world. Spotlighting progress like this, to the largest possible audience, is why the Earthshot prize exists,” Swift said.

This article was amended on 9 November 2026. An earlier version said the Vantara zoo covers 400 hectares; in fact, it is 1,400 hectares.

#130. Here’s the latest.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said old equipment was overloaded, causing “instability” in radar communications. Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin says there is no indication yet of a cyberattack but did not rule it out.

The nationwide ground stop that brought all air travel to a halt across the country and stranded thousands of passengers has been canceled, according to the Federal Aviation Administration.

Within minutes of flight traffic being restored, Sean Duffy, the transportation secretary, took to a podium Friday night at the White House to decry Congress, accusing lawmakers of dragging their feet when it came to fully funding upgrades he warned were vital to prevent similar failures from happening again.

“It’s going to continue to fail,” Mr. Duffy told reporters, adding: “How many times does this have to happen for Congress to give the money to fix it?”

Mr. Duffy added that the antiquated radar and telecoms systems that controllers rely on to transmit data and other information between facilities began to falter at 3:36 p.m. The aging system, which has undergone a number of upgrades, became overloaded, officials said.

Appearing alongside him, Markwayne Mullin, the secretary of homeland security, stressed that there was never any indication that the communications failures had been triggered by a cyberattack, but he added, “we are still looking, and we’re not going to rule it out.” He emphasized that lives were never in danger.

But the incident appeared to inject new fuel into Mr. Duffy’s now monthslong campaign to secure at least $30 billion in extra funding for air traffic control modernization.

Last year, Congress approved $12.5 billion to upgrade aging air traffic facilities and technology, funds Mr. Duffy has repeatedly referred to as a “down payment” when asking for tens of billions more. On Friday night, Mr. Duffy appeared to up the ante, telling reporters that he didn’t just want another $30 billion for the project, but another $50 billion. He argued that it should be delivered in emergency funding before the end of the year, after which control of Congress — and the budget — switches from Republicans to Democrats.

Some Democratic lawmakers have already raised questions about how efficiently and effectively the F.A.A. has spent the money they have been given thus far. But Mr. Duffy has long argued that his department cannot be expected to complete a meaningful overhaul of the nation’s air traffic control system without a massive influx of funding.

Friday’s nationwide ground stop was only the third that the F.A.A. has issued this century. It ranks as the second-longest, after the ground stop issued on Sept. 11, 2001, in the wake of the terrorist attacks on the World Trade Center in New York. There was a shorter one on Jan. 11, 2023, because of an outage in the F.A.A.’s system to alert pilots to safety issues.

The order on Friday halted nearly all departures and stranded thousands of passengers at airports across the country. Hundreds of flights were diverted to other airports and more were canceled, even as some internationally bound flights managed to secure permission to take off. Amid the delays and cancellations, many passengers were taken off flights or told to go home.

More than 2 million people pass through airport security checkpoints on a typical day, according to Transportation Security Administration data. On Thursday, the agency screened nearly 2.5 million people. According to FlightRadar24, an aviation tracking site, there were more than 1,000 airborne aircraft bound for U.S. airports during the nationwide ground stop. According to the flight tracking app Flighty, there were more than 30,000 departures and arrivals scheduled for the remainder of the day.

Passengers waiting to take off Friday afternoon were stuck in limbo.

In the Denver airport, passengers were taken off their flights with their suitcases. A handful began rebooking travel for the next day. But most waited patiently for any kind of update. A Southwest flight attendant announced that the airline expected an update in 30 minutes.

Before the advisory was published, the F.A.A. halted flights to Chicago’s O’Hare and Midway airports, citing equipment problems, and reported equipment-related departure delays at Newark, Las Vegas, Teterboro, Orlando, Houston and Phoenix.

If your flight is canceled: You are entitled to a refund if your flight is canceled in the United States, regardless of the reason, or if a flight is significantly delayed or changed and you decide not to take it. Here are other travel tips that might be helpful. Read more ›

A busy travel month: The ground stop happened about two weeks before Thanksgiving, one of the busiest times of the year for air travel. On average, U.S. airlines are expected to fly about 2.7 million passengers each day over the holiday period, according to industry trade group Airlines for America.

Handling the chaos: Radio transmissions between air traffic controllers and pilots at major airports offered a peek at how they navigated the ground stoppage. Read more ›

The last ground stop: The last nationwide ground stop came in the early morning of Jan. 11, 2023, and lasted around 90 minutes. The disruption came after a system to alert pilots of real-time dangers went down. Read more ›

Christine Chung, Niraj Chokshi, Luke Broadwater, Dustin Volz, Lisa Lerer and Robert Chiarito contributed reporting.

The Federal Aviation Administration issued a rare nationwide ground stop Friday afternoon, after outdated air traffic communications equipment became overloaded with data and began to fail, officials said.

The outage stranded thousands of passengers and caused widespread delays from which airlines and airports are still struggling to recover. It also highlighted persistent vulnerabilities in the aging U.S. air traffic infrastructure and a funding fight, as administration officials demanded lawmakers approve tens of billions of dollars more to accelerate systemwide upgrades.

Sean Duffy, the transportation secretary, told reporters at the White House Friday night that the radar and other air traffic communications started to falter at 3:36 p.m. when old routers became overloaded with data. While the failures were not universal, they were pervasive enough to prompt officials to issue the unusual nationwide moratorium on takeoffs, Mr. Duffy said.

Appearing alongside him at the White House, Markwayne Mullin, the homeland security secretary, stressed that there was no evidence that nefarious activity had triggered the technology failures.

“We’ve seen zero evidence that this was a cyberattack,” Mr. Mullin said, suggesting that new equipment and new technology the Mr. Duffy’s department had added to the old infrastructure was what had overwhelmed it.

Mr. Duffy insisted that the failure was caused by the antiquated, legacy communications equipment, not recent upgrades to the system.

“What we have is an incredibly old air traffic control system,” Mr. Duffy said, predicting that until significant upgrades are made, “it’s going to continue to fail.”

Mr. Duffy warned that it would take “some time” for air travel nationwide to recover from the ground stop, predicting flight schedules would be fully back to normal by the time the Thanksgiving travel season begins.

“I believe that it’s going to take us some time to get back to capacity,” he said, adding: “We’re going to have some makeup to do.”

Mr. Duffy said that the F.A.A. would prioritize larger commercial flights ahead of private and charter flights, to get previously grounded flights off the ground.

Mr. Duffy has long been pushing for a sweeping overhaul of the U.S. air traffic infrastructure, routinely complaining that controllers have been hampered by the country’s failure to upgrade outdated equipment.

“We have old, old switches, old routers — we use 1980s little copper lines,” he said to reporters Friday. Only last week, the F.A.A. announced that it had begun to phase out the use of floppy disks.

Last year, Congress approved $12.5 billion to fund upgrades to the country’s aging air traffic facilities and networks, some of which has been directed toward communications system upgrades. Mr. Duffy noted Friday that air traffic facilities that had been upgraded to digital systems did not experience the same overloading issues that prompted the F.A.A. to issue the ground stop order.

But he stressed that having an air traffic control system dependent on a “patchwork” of upgrades and fixes was untenable, calling on Congress to spend tens of billions more to allow for more upgrades.

For months, Mr. Duffy has been calling on the Republican-controlled Congress to give his department another $30 billion to accelerate efforts to upgrade air traffic control systems. On Friday night, he upped the ante, telling reporters he wanted $50 billion — and that he wanted it before the end of the year.

“This is an emergency, I want Congress to give us emergency funding,” he said, adding: “How many more times does this have to happen for Congress to give the money to fix it?”

But it is unclear whether Republicans will prioritize such a sum before the end of the year, after which control of Congress shifts to the Democrats, who are expected to scrutinize how the F.A.A. has utilized air traffic funding they have received so far. Republican lawmakers have filed bills in the House and Senate to authorize $30 billion in air traffic upgrades, but neither piece of legislation has cosponsors.

Later on Friday, Representative Hakeem Jeffries of New York, the House’s top-ranking Democrat, appeared to reject Mr. Duffy’s accusing tone and shifted the blame back to the Trump administration.

“Today’s unprecedented breakdown is the latest in a series of unacceptable air travel incidents under the Trump administration’s failed leadership,” he said in a statement, adding that “American travelers deserve timely answers from the Trump administration about how and why this happened and assurances that steps are being taken to ensure that our air travel system remains safe and functional.”

Nationwide, more than 8,200 flights — or over 31 percent of those scheduled in the United States for Friday — were canceled, according to Cirium, an aviation data firm. And the disruption was already spilling over into Saturday, usually a slower day for travel, with more than 5 percent of flights canceled before the day had even begun.

Reporting from Denver, where they are stranded along with much of the Democratic Party.

Pete Buttigieg could not believe what he had just watched. The former transportation secretary stood at the corner of the hotel bar in Denver, where CNN had just finished broadcasting the Trump administration’s news conference about the ground stop that stopped flights nationwide on Friday.

Mr. Buttigieg was among the thousands of Democrats who had gathered in Denver this week for a conference designed to chart the party’s future and celebrate their midterm wins. The ground stop stranded some of the party’s top rising stars and their staffs.

People accustomed to having inside information suddenly had very little.

At a table to Mr. Buttigieg’s left sat Gov. Abigail Spanberger of Virginia, Gov. Mikie Sherrill of New Jersey and Senator Elissa Slotkin of Michigan. They munched on Parmesan fries and checked their phones as they waited for news of their flights.

To his right, Senator Raphael Warnock of Georgia scanned his Senate group chats to check for updates from secure channels and Senator Mark Warner of Virginia.

But Mr. Buttigieg, glancing up at the television, struggled to comprehend the performance of his successor, Secretary Sean Duffy.

“You can just write down the comic book asterisk swearing characters,” Mr. Buttigieg said when a New York Times reporter asked for his impressions of the news conference. “I have questions.”

The downtown hotel bar resembled the famous bar scene in “Star Wars.” The stranded politicians bemoaned the lack of information from the federal government and their inability to get home.

One New England-based operative booked a rental car and planned to drive back to Boston. Others looked up how many hours it would take to get to the East Coast on Amtrak. (The answer: more than 40.) Aides to one prominent Democratic governor, who had flown to Denver on a private jet, fielded questions from others in the bar about whether they could hitch a ride home.

A few people working for Gov. JB Pritzker of Illinois — who did not come to Denver — commandeered a long table where Anne Caprara, his chief of staff, worked two phones as she juggled rumors, emergencies back home and calls from the boss. She said Mr. Pritzker was furious about the lack of information coming from the Trump administration.

“Welcome to Denver’s hottest bar, the Mos Eisley Cantina of politics,” Ms. Caprara said, referring to the “Star Wars” bar. “With ‘Veep’ characters and a ‘Terminator 2’ vibe.”

Representative Hakeem Jeffries of New York, the leader of the House Democrats, shifted the blame for Friday’s ground stop back to the Trump administration. In a statement, he called the stop “the latest in a series of unacceptable air travel incidents under the Trump Administration’s failed leadership.”

Earlier Friday night, Sean Duffy, the transportation secretary, had blamed Congress for not adequately funding his department’s plans to upgrade the nation’s air traffic infrastructure.

“American travelers deserve timely answers from the Trump Administration about how and why this happened,” Mr. Jeffries added.

A communications outage on Friday led the Federal Aviation Administration to pause departing flights nationwide, but pilots were not entirely cut off from federal air traffic controllers.

In a Friday night news conference, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said the outage was intermittent, so federal controllers were still able to reach pilots.

“It wasn’t a complete blackout in communication or radar,” he said. “So we were able to keep some of those communications going.”

But even in a complete outage, aviation authorities have multiple backups to maintain contact with pilots.

Pilots and air traffic controllers primarily communicate by voice radio, using primary, backup and emergency frequencies.

Controllers and airline dispatchers can also send text-based messages to pilots. And many commercial planes are equipped with satellite communications, which are especially useful for reaching people on the ground when the planes are flying in remote airspace like over the ocean.

If a control tower loses radio capabilities, it can also communicate with pilots with high-intensity colored lights, which pilots can acknowledge by rocking their wings or flashing lights in response.

Airlines began to restore some flights after the nationwide stop was lifted. United Airlines said that while many of its flights had been canceled, some were still expected to operate.

American Airlines said it was prioritizing flights to non-hub airports and international destinations in order to get planes and crews in place to recover quickly. Southwest Airlines said it was resuming a “limited number of flights” on Friday.

Some professional and college sports teams were stuck on runways, or opting to pile into buses, ahead of games this weekend that are now uncertain due to the nationwide ground stop that was lifted Friday night.

Markwayne Mullin, the secretary of homeland security, said at a news conference on Friday that the ground stop was caused by technology upgrades “overwhelming” the flight communication system. Thousands of flights have been canceled, but no high-profile games have been affected so far, ahead of what is set to be a busy weekend in sports.

Dozens of teams had made it out of airports just ahead of the stop. But the N.B.A.’s Brooklyn Nets were not so lucky.

The Athletic reported that the Nets were stuck on a team plane on Friday. They have a game Saturday night against the Memphis Grizzles.

Also stuck at an airport was the University of Texas Longhorns football team, slated to play Louisiana State University in Baton Rouge, La., on Saturday night. Minutes before the stoppage, the Longhorns posted a video of players boarding a plane with the caption “Bayou bound.”

Both teams are in the Southeastern Conference, which said in a statement that it would work “to determine whether any adjustments to scheduled competitions become necessary.”

On Friday night, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy told reporters that he encouraged college football teams to coordinate their flights with the Federal Aviation Administration “to make sure we have games going off tomorrow.”

While some teams, including the Longhorns, headed home from the airport to wait out the delay, a handful of others opted to travel the old-fashioned way: By bus.

The Athletic reported that among the longest drives was a 330-mile ride for the Purdue University Boilermakers, who are playing the University of Iowa Hawkeyes on Saturday night.

The University of Southern California women’s basketball team said they were grounded in Los Angeles ahead of a marquee game Sunday against the University of South Carolina Gamecocks, but were preparing to travel on Saturday.

In men’s college basketball, a Saturday game between University of North Carolina Greensboro and Cornell University in Ithaca, N.Y., was postponed because of the ground stop.

N.F.L. teams typically travel on Saturday for Sunday games. Five teams told The Athletic that their travel plans were not affected.

According to The Athletic, the stakes of the ground stop may be most significant for the National Women’s Soccer League, which is scheduled to hold its semifinals this weekend. The stop affected the Kansas City Current’s flight to New York City ahead of its game against Gotham FC on Sunday. The Utah Royals, playing the Washington Spirit on Saturday, traveled before the stoppage.

The N.B.A.’s Cleveland Cavaliers had planned to catch a flight home after their Friday night game in Miami. Kenny Atkinson, the Cavaliers head coach, told reporters before the game that he didn’t know if the team would make their flight.

“I think we’re scrambling to find hotel rooms in case,” Mr. Atkinson said, adding: “If not, we’ll sleep on the bus.”

Alexander CardiaNeil Bedi and Robin Stein

Radio transmissions between active pilots and air traffic controllers at major airports offer a peek at how they navigated the Federal Aviation Administration’s nationwide ground stoppage on Friday.

An air traffic controller at Newark Liberty International Airport can be heard sharing the news with pilots.

Minutes later, at airports in Atlanta and Chicago, controllers said they had limited information.

As planes landed over the next hour and departures were stuck at their gates, air traffic controllers can be heard talking about significant gate issues and comparing the tarmac to a “parking lot.”

Amid the chaos, one pilot noted the date while talking to a Newark controller: “It’s not lost on me that it’s Friday the 13th.”

Passengers groaned loudly as an announcement at Newark Liberty International Airport ordered them back to their gates for both domestic and international flights. “Are you kidding me? When will this end?” one woman cried out.

At Kennedy International Airport in New York, passengers waited to board a flight to Paris that was delayed because the pilot hadn’t been able to take his flight from Boston to J.F.K. Instead, he decided to drive, and passengers have been receiving updates about his E.T.A. over the loudspeaker. The latest update said he was expected to arrive at the airport at 9:45 p.m. When it was announced that the flight would begin boarding before his arrival, one woman raised both hands over her head with her fingers crossed.

More than 6,600 flights on Friday had been canceled by 9:30 p.m. Eastern, according to Flight Aware, a flight-tracking service. And that disruption had already spilled over into the weekend, with about 1,500 flights on Saturday canceled as well.

Pete Buttigieg, who served as the transportation secretary during the Biden administration, is stranded in Denver and mingling in the bar at the Ritz-Carlton hotel. After watching the Trump administration’s press conference, Buttigieg seemed exasperated. “You can just write down the comic book asterisk swearing characters,” Buttigieg said. “I have questions.”

It could take days for air travel to fully normalize after an event of this scale, said Vikrant Vaze, a Dartmouth professor specializing in commercial aviation logistics. “It’s going to take a lot of gymnastics” to get every airline’s aircraft, passengers and crew members back in the correct positions, he said.

Some contingency measures can be taken by airlines to get things back on track, such as flying reserve crews or “ferrying” an empty aircraft. It’s helpful that airlines have the nighttime hours, when there are typically fewer domestic flights, to push through some of the backlog, Vaze said.

Cheers and applause erupted in the Delta Sky Club at Los Angeles International Airport, where travelers just heard an announcement that the nationwide ground stop had been lifted. At least three Delta flights at the airport were already boarding, but it could take some time for other flights to get going, a Delta agent said in the announcement.

The White House briefing by the cabinet secretaries is now over.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy appeared to suggest that his effort to upgrade air traffic control infrastructure may have contributed to the outage, but he defended the upgrades by arguing that they had to be done.

“By doing nothing, you inject way more risk into the system,” he said, adding, “I didn’t anticipate that we would have an outage like this.”

Duffy has criticized Congress throughout this briefing for not allocating more funding to air traffic infrastructure.

“I can’t build things if I don’t have money,” Duffy said, shaking his head. “We should talk to our senators and members of Congress to say, ‘We don’t deserve this.’”

Sean Duffy has been clamoring for another $30 billion in air traffic modernization funds from Congress for several months now. On Friday night, he appeared to up the ante, saying he didn’t want just $30 billion — he wanted $50 billion. But the official ask, which he has made repeatedly and which is reflected in legislation he has endorsed, is another $30 billion.

In the last few weeks, Republicans in the House and Senate filed bills to give Duffy that $30 billion. Neither has any co-sponsors.

Asked if travelers should expect disruptions to their Thanksgiving travel plans, Sean Duffy said he thought flights would be “back up and operational as we come into the heat of travel” ahead of the holiday.

In the aftermath of a frightening air-traffic control technology failure that affected Newark Liberty International Airport in April 2025, Duffy argued that the nation’s air-traffic control systems were outdated and in need of a $12.5 billion upgrade. Tonight he is using the latest technology failure as an argument for another huge increase of funding.

Homeland Security Secretary Markwayne Mullin said that new technology upgrades overloaded the existing, antiquated system, “overwhelming” the existing equipment.

“It’s going to continue to fail,” Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said of the antiquated technology that the F.A.A. was trying to upgrade, triggering the telecom failures that prompted the nationwide ground stop.

The question of older F.A.A. systems ceasing to work has been a constant throughout Duffy’s tenure as transportation secretary, going back to disputes he had with Elon Musk about what type of technology could be used for backup systems.

The Congress that Duffy has been criticizing has been entirely in Republican hands for the last two years. It’s now about to switch to Democratic control.

“Our fix gives us a pretty good indication that it was a legacy network problem that went down,” Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said, hinting at the origins of the crisis. He said the problem was related to old equipment being overloaded.

Duffy and Mullin were asked if they could say with “100 percent certainty” that this was not the result of a cyberattack or rogue artificial intelligence. “There’s nothing certain right now,” Mullin said. “It doesn’t appear that way.”

“I want Congress to give us emergency funding,” Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said, calling on lawmakers to give the Trump administration another $30 billion, at least, to pay for further upgrades to air traffic control infrastructure. But some lawmakers have raised questions about how the F.A.A. has managed the funds it has been given thus far.

Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said the Federal Aviation Administration reopened airspace at 8:15 p.m. Eastern. But he said that flight departures would be slow, and he urged passengers to check with their airlines.

Speaking at a White House briefing, Transportation Secretary Sean Duffy said there were limits to how quickly flights could begin moving again tonight. “Hopefully tomorrow, we are going to start to see far better movement,” he said.

#129. Grenades, grief and a deepening political divide: five takeaways from Sonia Gandhi’s new memoir

From the assassinations of her husband and mother-in-law to press stonewalling under Modi, Belonging offers an insider’s account of India’s political evolution

Don’t get This is India delivered to your inbox? Sign up here

Few love stories have been as closely entwined with a nation’s history as that of Sonia and Rajiv Gandhi. The former’s much-awaited political memoir, Belonging, offers a strikingly candid account of her journey from a small town in Italy to the highest echelons of power in India – all for love, she says.

The veteran Congress party leader has been fiercely protective of her personal world. In Belonging, she opens up for the first time about the extraordinary twists and turns of a life that has largely unfolded in full public view. (The Guardian’s Hannah Ellis-Petersen has a rare in-person interview with Gandhi, and you can read an excerpt from the book here.)

Belonging, published today, contains moving glimpses of Gandhi’s private grief. She writes about the loss of her first child, who died shortly after birth, and the chilling discovery of grenades in a room of their house after Operation Blue Star, the 1984 military operation against Sikh separatists at the Golden Temple in Amritsar. She also provides a ringside view of several turbulent chapters in Indian politics, not least the violent assassinations of her mother-in-law, Indira Gandhi, in 1984 and her husband, Rajiv, in 1991.

Her book also revisits some of the darker episodes that have dogged the Gandhi family and the Congress party, including Indira’s imposition of the Emergency in June 1975, a 21-month period during which civil liberties were suspended, political opponents were jailed and the press was gagged, which caused fear and dismay across the country. In Gandhi’s account, it also caused a rift within the family.

Ultimately, Gandhi’s memoir is a vivid record of six decades of India’s political transformation from the Congress-dominated secular republic she moved to in 1968 until today, when Hindu nationalism has become the dominant political force under Narendra Modi.

Here are five key takeaways from her memoir and what they reveal about the state of Indian politics today.

Gandhi has had no real contact with Modi for the last 12 years

Gandhi’s account offers a glimpse of how deep the political polarisation runs in Indian politics today. In one revealing passage, Gandhi writes that she has had “practically no real contact” with Modi in the 12 years he has led the nation. In fact, at times when the two have been in the same rooms, such as the speaker’s small chamber, Modi “tends to look away, avoiding eye contact”, she says. On occasion, she adds, he has asked briefly about her health after a hospital stay.

For her, this breakdown is a departure from the past, when leaders on opposing sides of the aisle “would treat one another with cordiality”. After the 2001 terror attack on parliament, Gandhi immediately called then-BJP prime minister AB Vajpayee to enquire about his safety, a gesture he appreciated.

Gandhi sees an increasingly authoritarian style of politics, in which those in power seek to “belittle and torment” opponents. For years, Gandhi’s son, Rahul, has been the subject of BJP’s ridicule, which calls him Pappu, a derisive term intended to portray him as inexperienced and naive. She adds that similar things are happening elsewhere in the world, an observation that can be read in the context of US President Donald Trump’s use of personal insults and ridicule against opponents.

It’s lonely at the top for India’s female politicians

India has an illustrious history of women occupying top leadership positions, long before many other democracies. But their rise has often come at a cost – that of loneliness in navigating a male-dominated political world.

In the book, Gandhi describes a 2012 conversation with Hillary Clinton on the countrys’ female leaders and their backgrounds. In her recollections of Indira, she shares that, despite wielding authority, Indira could feel besieged and profoundly alone, and this was particularly acute, Gandhi writes, in the months leading up to the declaration of the Emergency by her mother-in-law.

Gandhi also writes of her own isolation, including when she decided to take on the leadership of the Congress out of a sense of duty to her family’s political legacy.

Those in power are increasingly stonewalling the press

Among the many ways in which Gandhi charts the erosion of India’s democratic conventions in Belonging, one particularly telling shift is the changed relationship between the media and those in power. She recalls a time when journalists had opportunities to question prime ministers directly.

In 12 years in power, Modi has not addressed a single press conference on his own. He has sat in on one press briefing at the conclusion of an election campaign, and appeared alongside US presidents at joint press encounters after bilateral meetings, where they took questions from the press. Modi has consistently avoided facing journalists in an open forum, and his media interactions have largely been one-on-one interviews with sympathetic, pro-government news anchors or in one much publicised exchange with a Bollywood actor who asked him how he liked to eat mangoes.

The book’s publication has brought renewed attention to concerns about the space for free expression in contemporary India, and the extent to which apprehensions about repercussions may influence what can be said, written and published.

Penguin Random House India was to publish Gandhi’s memoir but withdrew from the project in August. Gandhi told the Guardian that it was “uneasy in general with the criticism of the government” and said it had dropped the book after she declined certain suggested edits. Penguin has disputed her account, saying that the suggestion it decided not to publish the book because of refused editorial changes “does not accurately reflect the circumstances”.

The book, released in the US by Alfred A Knopf, is being published in India by HarperCollins.

In June, Penguin had also pulled out of plans to distribute graphic journalist Joe Sacco’s The Once and Future Riot, about the 2013 Muzaffarnagar communal violence. The publisher cited an inaccurate map of India and unresolved citation issues. Sacco, however, blamed the decision on the “current political climate in India”.

She’s had quiet fallouts with the ‘thoroughly dishonest’

Western political memoirs, particularly by former heads of government or senior politicians, are often forthcoming in naming political adversaries, recounting private exchanges and offering candid assessments of colleagues. David Cameron’s 2019 memoir, For the Record, took aim at his former friend, Michael Gove, describing him as “mendacious” and a “foam-flecked Faragist”.

Niha Masih takes a look at the stories, ideas and news makers of modern India

Gandhi, in contrast, is restrained. She writes warmly of the closeness between the Gandhi family and that of Bollywood superstar Amitabh Bachchan, whose parents she lived with when she first moved to India, but she makes no mention of the subsequent estrangement between the two families. In another instance, she does not name the political leader she describes as “thoroughly dishonest” and who she says she trusted in 1999, when Congress unsuccessfully attempted to form a coalition government.

Art | Art Mumbai runs from 12-15 November at the Mahalaxmi Racecourse. Among the highlights of this year’s edition is artist Baaraan Ijlal’s collaboration with the celebrated lyricist Javed Akhtar, turning his evocative Urdu couplets into paintings. The two will be in conversation with curator Ranjit Hoskote on Friday.

Government | The official bungalow of Andhra Pradesh’s chief minister, N Chandrababu Naidu, was renovated by a government-owned enterprise for Rs 6.5 crore (£518,000), according to the Indian Express (£). The renovation work on the Delhi property included was said to have had a major makeover.

Film | Bollywood’s ambitious rendering of Hindu epic Ramayana, starring Ranbir Kapoor and Sai Pallavi, was released on 5 November. Touted as the most expensive Indian film ever made, it earned praise from the Guardian’s Peter Bradshaw, who described it as “thoroughly engaging, rousing and spectacular”.

Readers from India and around the world on the festival of lights and tell us how they celebrated.

double quotation markIn India, Diwali comes to you. You can see it in the streets, hear it in the neighbourhood and feel it in the atmosphere. In the UK, it is different. Life carries on around you. But traditions do not have to disappear when you move countries – they can change shape. A small light in a quiet room can carry the same meaning as an entire street illuminated for Diwali. Aman, Plymouth, UK

double quotation markMy favorite part of Diwali back home was getting up before sunrise on Narak Chaturdashi and having a ceremonial bath, followed by a breakfast of traditional Marathi Diwali snacks. I have been living abroad for over 10 years now and it’s always a bittersweet time of the year where I miss my family and the general vibe of Diwali in India. But I am trying to build new traditions as one half of an Indian-Austrian couple, which includes trading the traditional boxes of mithai for Manner Schnitten and marzipan. Charu, Germany

double quotation markThere used to be more of a community spirit to Diwali. As kids, we would be tasked with going door to door, exchanging sweets and snacks with neighbours (sometimes to our great displeasure, because it made for awkward conversation with those we had annoyed with our antics). Nowadays, the celebrations are mostly private. Group celebrations are walled off within exclusive clubs, families light their own fireworks almost competitively, and people send each other sweets through a delivery service. Samyuktha, Chennai

The government has approved the sale of a 60.7% stake in IDBI bank to Canadian Fairfax Holdings for Rs 52,878 crore (£4.22bn).

It’s hard to take one’s eyes off Muhammed Riswan when he’s at work. The 23-year-old football freestyler from Areekode in Kerala’s Malappuram district conjures tricks with the ball in a delicately choreographed dance. It glides between his feet, next it is rolling on to his back right before he leaps into the air to catch it with one hand.

Riswan, who’s been freestyling for eight years, is one of the two Indians to win Instagram Rings 2026 award recognising creators. The other is Chanel model Bhavitha Mandava.

Riswan’s big break came with a video filmed at Keralamkundu waterfall, where he pulled off a freestyle move before gracefully arching the ball into the water. The clip has since clocked more than 600m views. Last year, he was invited to watch his sporting idol, Lionel Messi, play in Argentina.

#127. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.
